   231	### Q4. How it fails
   232	
   233	**F20 (CONFIRMED) Negation is a documented weakness of bi-encoders.** NevIR (arXiv 2305.07614, 2023, EACL 2024): "cross-
   234	encoders perform best, followed by late-interaction models, and in last place are bi-encoder and sparse neural
   235	architectures. We find that most information retrieval models (including SOTA ones) do not consider negation, performing
   236	the same or worse than a random ranking." URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.07614. It tests ranking two documents that
   237	differ only by a negation, with 2023 models; I found no newer measurement for current embedding models (listed below).
   238	For project memory this matters for entries such as "do not use X" next to "use X" (INFERRED).
   239	
   240	**F21 (CONFIRMED + INFERRED) Exact identifiers and rare terms.** Anthropic (F7): "they can miss crucial exact matches".
   241	EnterpriseRAG-Bench authors give a reason for vector's weak showing: "embedding models are trained on public corpora
   242	(web pages, Wikipedia, forums) and have limited exposure to enterprise-specific vocabulary such as project codenames"
   243	(quoted from the paper's Section 6.1, sentence continues "and CRM records, and conversational styles typical of internal messaging"; see F4 URL). The homelab report (F10, UNPROVEN)
   244	gives the concrete case: "find me the exact string TICKET-4471". For this project, entry ids (`D-`, `F-`, `L-`, `R-`) and
   245	`covers:` code paths are exact strings (INFERRED), which is the case where F7 and F10 say keyword search is the better tool.
   246	I found no measurement on code-symbol lookup specifically (GAP).
   247	
   248	**F22 (CONFIRMED + INFERRED) Superseded entries returned as if current.** Evidence is thin and points two ways:
   249	- F2: the BM25 free-text condition returned the superseded record "on the one pair where the superseded record outranks the
   250	  current one", current in 8% of trials; the vector tool was current on the reversal it was tested on.
   251	- F4: "Conflicting Info (20)": vector 60.0 correctness against BM25 90.0 and bash agent 80.0 (conflict handling over a
   252	  500,000-document corpus).
   253	Whether a superseded entry ranks above its replacement is a property of the text, not of the retriever (INFERRED from F2).
   254	In this design an index over `entries/` would hold whatever files are in the directory unless it filters on the `status`
   255	field; `INDEX.md` lists "active" entries only (SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md line 100). A vector index built over `entries/`
   256	without that filter would return superseded entries; one built over the same active set as `INDEX.md` would not
   257	(INFERRED; no source tested this).
   258	
   259	**F23 (CONFIRMED) Top-k cannot enumerate or prove absence.** F1 quote: it "cannot enumerate a complete set, establish an
   260	absence, or traverse relationships". Applies to any ranked top-k, vector or keyword (F1's notes-search column).
   261	
   262	**F24 (CONFIRMED) Drift between files and vectors; what the tools do about it.**
   263	- qmd: `update` reports "unchanged" and "removed" counts and "needsEmbedding" (new text not yet embedded); `embed` is a
   264	  second step, so between the two steps changed files have no current vectors (INFERRED from F14's API). A model change
   265	  invalidates every vector and needs `embed -f`.
   266	- Basic Memory: `bm reindex --embeddings` "does not discover new files on disk" (F15), i.e. two rebuild paths that can
   267	  disagree if one is skipped.
   268	- Claude Context: Merkle-tree change detection (F16, vendor claim).
   269	- No tool README I opened states a "never stale" guarantee for the vector side, and none exposes a "stale" flag on a
   270	  returned hit (INFERRED: I did not find one).
   271	- The code-graph analogue is a measured silent drift (s3-F25: "4.3% of distinct edges were wrong"; s3-F27: a CLI "returned
   272	  'No results found'" for a newly added symbol). I found no equivalent measurement for a vector index from files (GAP).
   273	  Practitioner advice (hash content not mtime; delete orphan chunks; canary edits; full re-embed on model change) is
   274	  UNPROVEN, from blogs such as https://tianpan.co/blog/2026-06-02-the-vector-index-whose-source-updates-never-reached-the-embeddings
   275	  and https://dev.to/futhgar/silent-drift-why-re-embedding-only-on-count-changes-rots-your-semantic-index-3k6h
   276	  (search-result descriptions only; I did not open them).
   277	
   278	**F25 (UNPROVEN) Claude Code dropping its vector index.** Many secondary write-ups repeat that a Claude Code lead said
   279	"Early versions of Claude Code used RAG + a local vector db, but we found pretty quickly that agentic search generally
   280	works better", citing staleness and security of an index. I could not open the primary post. Not relied on.
   281	
   282	### Q5. What fits here: smallest working versions (options, no choice)
   283	
   284	Machine: Python 3.12, SQLite 3.45.1 with FTS5, numpy 2.4.3, RTX 4070 Ti SUPER 16 GB, Node 22.22.1, ~700 GB free; no
   285	embedding runtime installed. The question for the corpus is that a project-memory `entries/` directory of hundreds of
   286	files is a few thousand vectors at most (INFERRED from F12 and 300-1,000 token chunks); brute-force search over that is
   287	trivial (author's sqlite-vec figure, F17, is 33ms for 1,000,000 x 128-d).
   288	
   289	- **Option 0: none.** `INDEX.md` + grep (+ the SQLite FTS cache from the sibling research). Supported by F1 (relevance
   290	  parity, no vector gain on enumeration/supersession), F3, F4, F8, F12. Cost: zero new tools.
   291	- **Option A: static embeddings, no GPU.** `pip install model2vec` + numpy; `potion-base-8M` (7.6M params, ~8-30 MB, F18);
   292	  store a `.npy` matrix plus a JSON of per-file content hashes under `.cache/`; cosine by numpy. Rebuild = re-embed the
   293	  files whose hash changed (INFERRED design detail). Quality of static models on decision-style text: GAP; the model
   294	  card reports MTEB averages that I did not extract.
   295	- **Option B: small ONNX model, CPU or GPU.** `pip install fastembed` (or `fastembed-gpu`) with `bge-small-en-v1.5`
   296	  (33M params, 384 dims, F18/F19), vectors in a numpy file or in `sqlite-vec` (F17, pre-v1, brute force). This is the
   297	  stack Basic Memory ships by default (F15).
   298	- **Option C: larger model on the GPU.** `sentence-transformers` (needs PyTorch + CUDA wheels) or llama.cpp/TEI serving
   299	  `Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B` (1.2 GB f16, 639 MB Q8_0 GGUF, code retrieval stated in its card) or `embeddinggemma-300m`
   300	  (gated, Gemma terms). Gives the bigger models; adds a heavy dependency for a few thousand vectors (INFERRED). The 1-4

/bin/bash -lc "nl -ba runs/20261012-memory-caches/nodes/sqlite-fts.claude.md | sed -n '42,115p'" in /home/smoke01/dev/ancient-games

    42	**F2 — CONFIRMED.** FTS5's documented behaviour that matters for this project.
    43	URL: https://sqlite.org/fts5.html (read 2026-10-12, no page date).
    44	Quotes: `"The unicode61 tokenizer, based on the Unicode 6.1 standard. This is the default."`; `"The better the match, the numerically smaller the value returned."` (bm25); `"The contents of columns qualified with the UNINDEXED column option are not added to the FTS index."`
    45	So header fields such as `kind`, `status`, `covers` can ride along as `UNINDEXED` columns or sit in a plain table next to the FTS table.
    46	
    47	**F3 — INFERRED from F1, F2 and my measurement F13.** Which questions an agent asks, split by what the store adds over `grep` plus `INDEX.md`:
    48	- *Ranked free-text recall* ("which entries talk about retry budget"): FTS5 adds an order (BM25) and snippets. grep returns an unranked list. `INDEX.md` (one line per active entry) already covers a title-level scan.
    49	- *Header-field queries* ("every active decision whose `covers` includes path X", "what supersedes Y"): a SQL `WHERE` over a table of header fields answers these. grep over `entries/` answers them with a pipeline of greps or a short script (F13 shows both returning the same hits). No source found measures an agent doing either.
    50	- *Backlinks, orphans, broken `supersedes` links*: needs a link table or a script; FTS5 itself does not provide it (the harness in F5 builds a separate `links` table).
    51	- *Semantic ("by meaning") questions*: FTS5 does not answer these; that is the vector cache's question, not this one.
    52	
    53	### 2. When it pays off (brief item 2)
    54	
    55	**F4 — CONFIRMED.** The one controlled scaling measurement found for BM25 against an agent using grep over files. The "File-System Agent" had `list_dir`, `grep` (fixed string, up to 30 matching paths) and `read_doc` (first 8,000 characters), 80 LLM calls per question; the benchmark is a general RAG corpus, not code or memory entries.
    56	URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26497 and https://arxiv.org/html/2607.26497v3 ("BM25 Wins at Scale", submitted 2026-07-29, v3 2026-07-31).
    57	Quotes: `"Around 10 million corpus tokens, BM25 overtakes it and leads at every larger shared tier"`; `"its sequential exploration costs 39 times more query tokens at the bedrock."` Numbers from the extract of the HTML: smallest tier 1,144 documents and 1.7M corpus tokens; tokens per question at that tier BM25 5.8K against File-System Agent 226K; at full corpus 5.8K against 895K (agent figure includes retries); BM25 leads "with a margin near 20 points at full scale." Also: the agent leads "at the smallest shared tiers."
    58	Limits: the extract gave the abstract's "roughly 10 million" and the smallest tier, not a per-tier table. The File-System Agent's grep returns paths only, not lines, which is weaker than `rg` with context. This is one paper, preprint.
    59	
    60	**F5 — INFERRED from F4 and F13.** Where this project sits against that crossover. The design says an entry is "the claim in one or two sentences, then Why:, then How to apply:", so about 1 KB each. My synthetic entries of that shape came to 1.06 MB per 1,000 entries (F13). At an assumed 4 characters per token (my assumption, not measured) that is about 0.27M tokens per 1,000 entries, so the 10M-token crossover in F4 would be near 37,000 entries, and even the 1.7M-token smallest tier in F4 would be near 6,000 entries. `project-memory/` does not exist yet in this repo (checked), so the real corpus size is unknown. The only count available: `docs/` and `runs/` hold 364 markdown files today. **No measurement exists of when FTS5 or BM25 beats grep for an agent on a corpus of a few hundred to a few thousand short entries.** That is a GAP, not a finding.
    61	
    62	**F6 — CONFIRMED, and it cuts the other way.** Grep matched or beat vector retrieval in a controlled agent study.
    63	URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15184 (submitted 2026-05-14), HTML https://arxiv.org/html/2605.15184v1.
    64	Quotes: `"inline grep exceeds inline vector for every harness–model pair"` (Sec. 4.1.3); `"We do not claim that grep “beats” vector in general."`; `"Lexical tools may be disproportionately helpful here because answers often license on verbatim spans."` Example cells from its Table 1: Claude Opus 4.6 in Claude Code, inline grep 76.7 against inline vector 75.0; GPT-5.4 in Codex CLI 93.1 against 75.9.
    65	Limits: LongMemEval conversational QA (116 questions), not code memory. It compares grep with vector, not with BM25. The extract reported no token or cost figures. Relevant here mainly to the vector cache; for the FTS store it shows grep is a strong baseline.
    66	
    67	**F7 — CONFIRMED (vendor's own small benchmark).** A grep baseline recalled almost everything but was less precise than a hybrid index.
    68	URL: https://github.com/rohitg00/agentmemory/blob/main/docs/benchmarks/2026-05-20-coding-agent-life-v1.md (2026-05-20).
    69	Quotes: `"grep baseline: R@5 = 0.967, P@5 = 0.227 — missed one gold session in one multi-gold question."`; `"The benchmark is small (15 questions) and gold-sparse."` Hybrid got R@5 1.000 and P@5 0.240; the author says P@5 "is at the math ceiling for this dataset."
    70	Limits: 15 fictional sessions, 15 queries, author's own project. A web-search summary of this page gave P@5 as 0.267; the page says 0.227, and I use the page. The page's own caveat says precision cannot separate the adapters.
    71	
    72	**F8 — UNPROVEN.** Claude Code's own default is grep-style search, not an index (commonly reported). I did not open a primary source for it, so it is not used above.
    73	
    74	### 3. Current tools that keep markdown as the source and build a SQLite index (brief item 3)
    75	
    76	All of these are 2026; none is measured on a corpus like ours. Rebuild time and size are listed only where a source gave them; the local numbers are in F13.
    77	
    78	**F9 — CONFIRMED. `monorepo-agents-harness` `kb-index.sh`.** Closest match to the brief: full rebuild, gitignored, grep fallback.
    79	URLs: https://github.com/atayahmet/monorepo-agents-harness/issues/25 (2026-10-07) and https://github.com/atayahmet/monorepo-agents-harness/pull/26 (merged 2026-10-08, commit `6a22b7e`, VERSION 0.4.0-rc.13).
    80	Quotes: `"Rebuild in full every time."`; `"`query` compares the index build time with the newest source mtime and rebuilds first if the index is stale."`; `"A stale index is worse than no index."`; if `sqlite3` is missing or lacks FTS5 it should `"print one warning and fall back to the current grep path."`; rebuild `"finishes in under a second"` at about 1.4k files. PR: `"a deterministic, self-healing SQLite FTS5 index over `knowledge/**/*.md`"`, built into a temp file then renamed over `knowledge/.index.sqlite`; derived columns `kind`, `workspace`, `task_slug`, `date` come from page shapes, not frontmatter. Tests: 38/38; removing self-heal caused 2 failures and removing atomic publish caused 4.
    81	Local: yes, `sqlite3` CLI and shell. Tokenizer: not named (so FTS5 default). Size on disk: not stated.
    82	
    83	**F10 — CONFIRMED. Scott Spence's `node:sqlite` + FTS5 markdown tool** (and its "wiki0" successor).
    84	URL: https://scottspence.com/posts/give-coding-agents-your-project-docs-with-node-sqlite-and-fts5 (2026-09-17).
    85	Quotes: `"One chunk per heading, so results point at a section, not a whole file."`; the index command starts with `db.exec('DELETE FROM chunks')` and reinserts everything; `"with a SQLite index that can be rebuilt any time for search, backlinks and facts."`; facts are `"decisions, requirements, risks and assumptions so they could be queried on their own."` Numbers: about 260 posts as 2,647 chunks in under half a second including Node startup; search about 50 ms; in a real project docs grew from 65 to 291 and agents called the CLI 1,122 times across 327 sessions. FTS5 table made with no tokenizer option (default). Staleness: not discussed; full rebuild each run. Index size: not given. No comparison with grep is made in the post.
    86	
    87	**F11 — CONFIRMED. memweave** (Python; hybrid BM25 + optional vectors).
    88	URL: https://towardsdatascience.com/memweave-zero-infra-ai-agent-memory-with-markdown-and-sqlite-no-vector-database-required/ (2026-04-16, by its author).
    89	Quotes: `"The database is always a derived cache — if you delete it, memweave rebuilds it from the files."`; `"losing the database is not data loss. Losing the files is."`; `"BM25 catches exact matches — error codes, config values, proper names"`. Change detection: SHA-256 on files and chunks. Default merge weight 0.7 vector, 0.3 BM25. No size, rebuild time, latency or benchmark number is given.
    90	
    91	**F12 — CONFIRMED (partly). Other tools, one line each.**
    92	- `ai-memory` (Rust): `"Plain markdown on disk as source of truth. SQLite is just a derived index."`; `"Persistent index in SQLite + FTS5. Native. No reindex on every restart."`; author calls it beta, a few weeks in personal use. Its comparison target `agentmemory` reportedly pays `"~5 minutes rebuilding from scratch"` on restart and slowed at about 10K observations. https://akitaonrails.com/en/2026/05/23/i-built-memory-system-for-coding-agents-ai-memory/ (2026-05-23). The 5-minute figure is the author's report about another tool.
    93	- `sqliteai/sqlite-memory`: `"Computes a hash of the content. If the same content was already indexed, it is skipped entirely."`; changed file: `"the old entry (chunks, embeddings, FTS) is atomically replaced."` Search needs embeddings (a local GGUF model or a remote key), so it is not FTS-only. No size, time or benchmark number; no page date. https://github.com/sqliteai/sqlite-memory
    94	- `qmd` (Node): FTS5 index `documents_fts`, vectors in sqlite-vec, default index at `~/.cache/qmd/index.sqlite`, `qmd update` re-indexes, `qmd search` is `"BM25 full-text search only"`; vector, rerank and query-expansion models are about 300 MB, 640 MB and 1.1 GB. Rebuild time and size not stated; BM25-only offline use not confirmed from the page. Its fixture "typically shows" bm25 about 0.50, vector about 0.70, hybrid about 1.00, on the repo's own example fixture. https://github.com/tobi/qmd (no date shown).
    95	- Basic Memory: `"all knowledge is represented in standard Markdown files and the database serves as a secondary index."`; the page names neither SQLite nor FTS5 and does not cover rebuild. https://docs.basicmemory.com/reference/technical-information (sample timestamps only, no page date). Search results elsewhere call the database SQLite (UNPROVEN, not opened).

   109	### 4. How it fails (brief item 4)
   110	
   111	**F14 — CONFIRMED.** SQLite itself warns about an FTS5 index drifting from its source, and gives a repair.
   112	URL: https://sqlite.org/fts5.html (no page date). Quote: the `rebuild` command `"first deletes the entire full-text index, then rebuilds it based on the contents of the table"`; `integrity-check` fails with `SQLITE_CORRUPT_VTAB` on discrepancies. The extract also says keeping an external-content table in step with its source table is the user's job.
   113	Limit, INFERRED: `integrity-check` compares the index with the table it indexes. It does not compare that table with the markdown files on disk. I found no SQLite feature that does, so file drift must be caught by the tool (F15). Not tested here.

    92	### Effective context: the research
    93	
    94	- **W-10 RULER** — CONFIRMED. https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.06654, 2024-04-09 (v3 2024-08-06). Quote: "While these models all claim context sizes of 32K tokens or greater"; "only half of them can maintain satisfactory performance at the length of 32K." 17 models. Old models.
    95	- **W-11 NoLiMa** — CONFIRMED. https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.05167, 2025-02-07 (v3 2025-07-09). Quote: "At 32K, for instance, 11 models drop below 50% of their strong short-length baselines." GPT-4o "from an almost-perfect baseline of 99.3% to 69.7%." Needles with little word overlap with the question (the case for memory entries phrased differently from the question).
    96	- **W-12 Lost in the middle** — CONFIRMED. https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.03172, 2023-07-06 (v3 2023-11-20, TACL). Quote: performance "significantly degrades when models must access relevant information in the middle of long contexts." Supports putting the key lines of an index first and last, not in the middle.
    97	- **W-13 Chroma context rot** — CONFIRMED. https://www.trychroma.com/research/context-rot, 2025-07-14. 18 models (Claude Opus 4/Sonnet 4/3.7/3.5/Haiku 3.5, o3, GPT-4.1 family, GPT-4o, Gemini 2.5/2.0, Qwen3 235B/32B/8B). Quote: "model performance consistently degrades with increasing input length." Quote: "performance degrades more quickly in input length with lower similarity needle-question pairs." LongMemEval subset (306 prompts): focused prompts about 300 tokens against full prompts about 113k tokens, "Performance was consistently higher on focused prompts"; exact accuracy only in charts (GAP for the numbers).
    98	- **W-14 Anthropic, Opus 4.6** — CONFIRMED. https://www.anthropic.com/news/claude-opus-4-6, 2026-02-05. Quote: Opus 4.6 "scores 76%, whereas Sonnet 4.5 scores just 18.5%" on MRCR v2 8-needle at 1M. Calls it "a qualitative shift in how much context a model can actually use." This is the best vendor number for "window is not effective context" for a Claude model, but Opus 4.6 is not the current generation (Opus 5.5 is).
    99	- **W-15 MRCR v2 across vendors** — REPORTED. https://yage.ai/share/long-context-benchmark-en-20260315.html, 2026-03-15, compiling vendor system cards and model cards. Its table: Opus 4.6 93.0% at 256K and 76.0% at 1M; Sonnet 4.6 90.3% / 65.8%; GPT-5.4 79.3% (128K–256K bin) and 36.6% (512K–1M bin); Gemini 3.1 Pro 84.9% at 128K and 26.3% at 1M; Gemini 3 Pro 45.4% at 256K and 24.5% at 1M. The post itself warns scores from different sources "cannot be directly compared." I did not open the vendor reports behind the GPT and Gemini cells.
   100	- **GAP:** no published long-context score found for the current generation (Claude Opus 5.5 / Sonnet 5.5 / Fable 5.1, GPT-6.x, Gemini 3.6/3.8, DeepSeek V4, MiniMax M3). All the effective-context numbers above are for older models. An example value for a 1M-window model today would have to say "Opus 4.6-era: 76% at 1M, 93% at 256K".
   101	- **W-31 Gemini** — CONFIRMED. https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/long-context (undated). Quote: "You can get ~99% on a single query, but you have to pay the input token cost every time you send that query." And with multiple needles, "the model does not perform with the same accuracy." No number given for multi-needle.
   102	
   103	What this gives the guide for "how big an index read at session start": the cited limits (W-1, W-2, W-3, W-4) are all 25 to 32 KB or 500 lines; the research says accuracy loss shows up from tens of thousands of tokens on older models and from a few hundred thousand on newer ones (W-10, W-11, W-14), and that a rule list loses adherence as it grows (W-16, W-17). All of that is far above a few KB. So an index of a few KB is not near any effective-context problem; the limits matter for cost and adherence, not for recall.
   104	
   105	---
   106	
   107	## 3. When retrieval beats loading everything
   108	
   109	- **V-F8 (cited, not redone):** Anthropic 2024: under 200,000 tokens, include the whole knowledge base. Still the only vendor threshold found.
   110	- **W-18 LongMemEval** — CONFIRMED. https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.10813 (abstract) and https://arxiv.org/html/2410.10813. Submitted 2024-10 (ICLR 2025). Quote: "commercial chat assistants and long-context LLMs showing a 30% accuracy drop". LongMemEval_S is "approximately 115k tokens per problem, with histories of about 50 sessions"; LongMemEval_M is 500 sessions, "around 1.5 million tokens." Long-context reading, oracle (only the right sessions) against S (everything): GPT-4o 0.870 vs 0.606 (30.3% drop); Llama 3.1 70B 0.744 vs 0.334 (55.1%); Llama 3.1 8B 0.710 vs 0.454 (36.1%); Phi-3 128k 14B 0.702 vs 0.380 (45.9%). So reading about 115K tokens of history, which fits in every window above, cost 30% to 55% of accuracy for those 2024 models.
   111	- **W-19 BEAM** — CONFIRMED. https://arxiv.org/html/2510.27246v1 (Tavakoli et al., ICLR 2026; v1 2025-10). 100 conversations up to 10M tokens, 2,000 questions. Table 1 average score, "Vanilla" (long context) / RAG:
   112	
   113	| Model | 100K | 500K | 1M | 10M |
   114	|---|---|---|---|---|
   115	| Llama-4-Maverick | 0.240 / 0.323 | 0.283 / 0.330 | 0.259 / 0.307 | 0.104 / 0.249 |
   116	| GPT-4.1-nano | 0.239 / 0.309 | 0.194 / 0.314 | 0.191 / 0.302 | 0.109 / 0.218 |
   117	| Gemini-2.0-flash | 0.242 / 0.280 | 0.257 / 0.267 | 0.199 / 0.271 | 0.122 / 0.216 |
   118	| Qwen2.5-32B-AWQ | 0.280 / 0.269 | 0.200 / 0.291 | 0.193 / 0.285 | 0.133 / 0.211 |
   119	
   120	  Quote: "even LLMs with long context windows perform substantially worse as conversation length increases." Read: retrieval already matched or beat loading at 100K in 3 of 4 models and beat it in all 4 from 500K (my reading of the table). Caveats from the paper: vanilla at 10M uses "the largest recent segment that fits" each window; the Qwen vanilla baseline used 128K while RAG used 32K. Models are 2025-era. The unit is conversation tokens, not entries.

   330	## What each trigger comparison would show for SEZA today (your reading)
   331	
   332	These are my readings from the numbers above. None is a run of the CACHES.md benchmark (BM-1..BM-9); there is no
   333	recorded-question set for SEZA, so the comparison is on my 5 questions only (n=5, chosen by me).
   334	
   335	| Trigger | Comparison in CACHES.md | What SEZA's numbers show (my reading) |
   336	|---|---|---|
   337	| R5.1 full-text | full-text reaches the right entry after reading less than `INDEX.md` plus grep, no more misses | The index here is 12,108 B (3,027 tokens), and it is already loaded at session start, so reading it costs nothing extra. In my test every index line for Q1-Q5 matched its pattern (Q2 only in the index). grep over the 55-file memory folder prints 361 to 3,348 B; over the 257-file larger set 617 to 14,203 B, except the broad term (Q5) 109,519 B.    432	That API result should not replace SEZA’s locally configured Codex budget.
   433	
   434	**When an index read stops fitting**
   435	
   436	There are three distinct boundaries:
   437	
   438	- **Whole-context capacity:** the formula above. A memory-entry count cannot determine it without the index’s characters per entry.
   439	- **Automatic loading caps:** Claude’s documented `MEMORY.md` cap is **200 lines or 25KB**, whichever comes first. Qwen’s installed source defines `MAX_MANAGED_AUTO_MEMORY_INDEX_LINES = 200` and `MAX_MANAGED_AUTO_MEMORY_INDEX_BYTES = 25e3` at [chunk-GXSNGY5M.js:84471](/home/smoke01/.npm-global/lib/node_modules/@qwen-code/qwen-code/chunks/chunk-GXSNGY5M.js:84471). These can bind long before model capacity.
   440	- **A tool’s advisory budget:** Qwen defines `MEMORY_CONTEXT_WARNING_RATIO = 0.15` at [chunk-GXSNGY5M.js:146045](/home/smoke01/.npm-global/lib/node_modules/@qwen-code/qwen-code/chunks/chunk-GXSNGY5M.js:146045). Its warning estimates tokens from character count and compares all always-loaded context against that fraction at [line 147724](/home/smoke01/.npm-global/lib/node_modules/@qwen-code/qwen-code/chunks/chunk-GXSNGY5M.js:147724). This is a software warning threshold, **not measured evidence of degradation**.
   441	
   442	Current agy documentation also specifies a “24 KB (`24,000` bytes) per-file limit” and a “20,000-token aggregate rules budget.” Over-budget rules may become pointers rather than inline text. [Antigravity rules](https://www.antigravity.google/docs/rules/), accessed 2026-10-12. **GAP:** verified behavior of those limits on SEZA’s installed CLI.
   443	
   444	Codex’s instruction cap and DSH’s instruction-loader byte budget apply if an index is placed in that instruction chain. An ordinary subsequent file read consumes model context but is not thereby governed by the same loader cap.
   445	
   446	For an illustration tied to SEZA’s actual index, the following arithmetic assumes a future index has the same average line density as Claude’s current index. **This extrapolation is mine, not a finding.** Its hypothetical entry counts are capacity illustrations, not cache-adoption triggers.
   447	
   448	**Measurement/arithmetic M4 — startup totals and index extrapolation**

   450	Command:
   451	
   452	```bash
   453	python3 - <<'PY'
   454	from pathlib import Path
   455	import math

    61	## Contested points
    62	
    63	**CP1 — The role of SQLite.**
    64	- **opencode:** SQLite *is* the structured state store — "structured state tracking (ticket status, session logs, entity relationships)", written to `.ai/state.sqlite` inside the repo and queried at session start for `status != done`. Basis: **model knowledge** (C2), justified as "lightweight, queryable, no external service needed, atomic writes".
    65	- **claude and codex:** SQLite is at most a **derived, gitignored, rebuildable cache** and never authoritative. claude places it at `.cache/` "rebuilt from the tree"; codex places it at `.cache/project-memory/index.sqlite` gitignored and requires that "its absence must not prevent reading authoritative memory", refusing a committed SQLite as sole memory because "it adds a required access mechanism where inspectable files already meet the proposed need". Basis: codex `read: https://sqlite.org/fts5.html` plus its **model-knowledge** recommendation; claude's T1/T2/T4 design tests.
    66	- **unresolved.** Nothing in the three files measures a committed binary state store against markdown state files. What would settle it: on one real project, track ticket/session state both ways and compare merge-conflict rate on the binary, the cost of querying state without the DB (does a reader still recover?), and the human reviewability of a `state.sqlite` diff.

    24	## By category
    25	
    26	`file hit@5` counts a gold `path::name` as hit when the route returns its file. `symbol hit@5` needs the symbol itself: an entry naming it, the top-level def/class holding it or a method of it, a module-code chunk for a module-level name, and for `rg` a printed match line inside the symbol's lines. A gold given as a plain file scores the same on both. **The best route is chosen on symbol hit@5.**
    27	
    28	`chars` is the mean number of characters of output read up to and including the first line where the route names a gold file as its answer (`# <path> (`, or a line starting `<path>:`), over the answers that named one; `n` is how many did. A miss is left out, never scored 0. What each route prints differs -- rg: a `# path (N matching lines)` header per file plus at most 40 match lines of up to 300 characters; fts: one 24-token snippet per path; vec: the first 300 characters per path; hybrid: the vec or fts block per path; graph: one id per line -- so `chars` measures the print format as much as the store.
    29

   175	> `codegraph affected [files...]`
   176	> `codegraph affected --stdin # pipe from git diff`
   177	> `AFFECTED = $( git diff --name-only HEAD …`
   178	
   179	Relevant to §6: **the vendor's graph consumes git's answer to "what changed"; it does not try to out-perform git at it.**
   180	
   181	**F18 — the in-repo tool.** `code_graph/code_graph.py`, 981 lines, stdlib only (`argparse, ast, builtins, fnmatch, json, sys, collections, pathlib`). Emits JSON to **stdout only** — no DB, no file, no cache, no lockfile, no incremental path; `GraphBuilder.run()` re-walks, re-reads and re-`ast.parse`s everything every invocation. `*.py` only (`self.root.rglob("*.py")`, `code_graph.py:262`). Node kinds `module`/`function`; edge kinds `contains`/`import`/`call`/`fixture` (`code_graph.py:55`). Its own stated design rule — "Files are walked in sorted path order. Two runs on the same tree give byte-identical output" — means it **cannot drift** (confirmed independently by F5's determinism control, below).
   182	
   183	---
   184	
   185	## 4. How it fails
   186	
   187	**F5 — CONFIRMED — this is the best-documented drift study I could find, and it is a vendor measuring itself against its own tool.** <https://github.com/colbymchenry/codegraph/blob/main/docs/benchmarks/index-drift-cg33.md> (last commit 2026-10-07; "Measured 2026-08-06"). Confirms and extends s3-F25/F26/F28.
   188	
   189	*Silent, and the README says the opposite.*
   190	
   191	> "A live, auto-sync-maintained index **does not converge** to a clean full rebuild of the identical working tree. On codegraph's own repo, **4.3% of distinct edges were wrong**, in both directions, overwhelmingly `calls` edges."
   192	> "This matters because it is silent: nothing warns, nothing surfaces it, and the README tells users the index is never stale and there is nothing to re-run."
   193	
   194	| | count |
   195	|---|---|
   196	| distinct edge triples (rebuild) | 28,809 |
   197	| in rebuild but **missing** from live | **751** |
   198	| in live but **absent** from rebuild (stale) | **476** |
   199	| **total divergent** | **1,227 — 4.3%** |
   200	
   201	*Failure mode 1 — a count-based check lies.* (New; not in r2.)
   202	
   203	> "Raw edge **rows** were 39,845 live vs 40,122 rebuilt — a benign-looking +0.7%. The divergence is bidirectional, so a net-count check nets it out and reports almost nothing wrong. **Any drift detector must compare edge sets, not totals.**"
   204	
   205	*Failure mode 2 — every conventional integrity check passes.* (New.)
   206	
   207	> "Node sets are identical — `files` 501 = 501, `nodes` 10,110 = 10,110, heuristic edges 36 = 36 — and every integrity check is 0 on *both* indexes: no duplicate nodes, no orphan edges, no nodes referencing a missing file row. **Nothing accumulates. Cross-file resolution goes stale.**"
   208	
   209	*Failure mode 3 — it is not noise.*
   210	
   211	> "Control, rebuild vs rebuild on the same tree and build: **0 differing edges** (28,809 both runs). So the live-vs-rebuild delta is not run-to-run noise."
   212	
   213	*Failure mode 4 — the fix is partial, by design.*
   214	
   215	> "| 80 commits | 1,634 (963 / 671) | 890 | **361 (359 / 2)** |"
   216	> "**The full rebuild is the wrong one here** — converging would mean teaching sync to manufacture thousands of wrong edges. Left as is, deliberately."
   217	
   218	*Failure mode 5 — a mistyped cache path is indistinguishable from staleness.* (New; directly relevant to M6.)
   219	
   220	> "`sqlite3` against a mistyped path **creates an empty database** rather than failing, and every subsequent query then answers from an empty schema — which reads exactly like a stale pre-migration index."
   221	
   222	*Failure mode 6 — retrieval quality decays invisibly (s3-F26, re-verified).*

# Synthesis — three reviews of the caches requirements

Written 2026-10-12 by the COO (Claude `claude-opus-5-5`) from `codex.md`, `opencode.md` and `claude.md` in this
folder. All three: **REPAIR**. Nothing has been changed in `docs/` from these reviews; that waits for EJ.

**Checked by me against the sources** (marked ✓): the S-F9 citation, the V-F13 miss definition, the five trigger
lines in `CACHES.md`, the sibling `INDEX.md` rules, M6's status, and the benchmark's miss rule. The rest is the
reviewers' reading, not re-checked.

-## Code graph
-
-### Five whys
-
-1. **Why a code graph?** To answer structural questions about the code, such as what an edit touches.
-2. **Why would memory need that?** For "which entries cover the files I am editing" (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` Rd4.1). The
-   drift check it once also served was removed by EJ on 2026-10-12.
-3. **Why is a text search enough?** `covers:` names **paths**, not symbols, and is already a hand-kept index from entry
-   to path (`G-F14`), so finding the entries that cover a file is a search over `covers:` lines.
-4. **Why not add a graph anyway?** An incrementally synced graph adds a silent failure that a text search does not have
-   (`G-F5 drift`, 4.3% of edges wrong). A full-rebuild graph such as the repo's own does not drift (`G-F18`), but it
-   covers Python only, so for other `covers:` paths it gives a silent partial answer (code-graph node §5, option B).
-   Whether an entry is still true is answered by reading it against the code (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` Rd5), not by a
-   graph.
-5. **Why might it be needed later?** Either of two conditions in the code-graph node's §6, each a test:
-   - `covers:` starts naming functions or classes instead of files;
-   - an agent must answer about files not on any `covers:` list ("what does this change affect").

 - It is never read at session start (`M18`).
 - It is never built before its own trigger fires. Every trigger is an observable comparison, and the measurement
   decides it, not a number set in advance (EJ, 2026-10-12: *"this is design guide, how can I give you a number?"*).
 - It never becomes a second thing to keep in sync: delete the folder and memory is unaffected.
 
-**How we know it works.** Two tests, both on the project's own recorded questions.
+**How we know it works.** Two tests, on the project's own questions and noted misses.
 - **It finds the right entry, for less.** Full-text search reaches the right entry after reading less than
   `INDEX.md` plus grep, with no more misses (B1.1). Vector search, run on the recorded misses, finds entries
   full-text did not, and does no worse on the other questions (B1.2).

codex
I’m withdrawing two of my findings: K2.3 does not establish that an unapproved vector index must be built, and “the same set as `INDEX.md`” does not make it the authority for cache membership. I also disagree with the claimed K3.2 circularity: K3 applies after a cache exists, so a full-text-only installation can record a miss and trigger a recheck.

codex
### Points I now support

Reader: **codex**. Positions below are **my reading** after checking all four round-1 reports against the sources. `CACHES.md` means the [review snapshot](/home/smoke01/dev/ancient-games/runs/20261013-caches-design-read/inputs/CACHES.md). This is a documentary review; I did not rerun measurements or change files.

1. **The main changes hold, subject to the dependent wording findings below — agree with all four readers.** Dropping the full question log, generating question sets when comparisons run, removing K3’s rebuild-cost clause, removing stored `status`, and moving the operational code-graph sections follow the recorded decisions. Deciding lines: `CACHES.md:20–22`, “only misses are noted”; `:411–412`, “the rebuild-cost part was dropped”; `:372–374`, “no row stores `status`” and “The headers are the reference”; `:134–136`, Part 2 moved while its evidence remains.

2. **Missing-model fallback contradicts Rd3.3 — agree with claude F1, codex F3 and agy F1.** At `CACHES.md:148–149`, “the same for a missing embedding model” inherits the preceding grep fallback. The deciding requirement at `:442–443` says: “falls back to full-text if it is there, and to grep if not.” Claude F12’s observation about the vector fallback row at `:89` also holds: that row omits available full-text. These summaries need the accepted fallback order.

3. **Rd4 excludes the journal note B1.1 needs — agree with claude F2, codex F2 and opencode F3.** B1.1 at `CACHES.md:327–328` runs when an agent notes that the baseline “made it read through many hits to reach an entry.” Rd4.1 at `:454` then says, “Every other question goes unrecorded.” A successful but expensive search need not satisfy B1.2’s miss definition. The contradiction concerns which exceptional searches get noted; it does not justify restoring a log of every search.

4. **Historical misses need a stated interpretation against the current active corpus — hold codex F4.** K3.1 at `CACHES.md:411` uses “the noted misses”; B4.1 at `:371` indexes active entries “and nothing else.” Rd4.2 at `:456–457` creates fresh questions from entries existing then, but does not explain what happens to a historical miss whose target has since become superseded. **Cannot tell** which treatment is intended. The missing interpretation should remain open rather than treating deliberate exclusion as a retrieval failure.

5. **“The B1.1 question set” is ambiguous after the change to fresh sets — agree with claude F8.** `CACHES.md:337` names that set, while Rd4.2 at `:456` says, “The question set for a comparison is built when the comparison runs.” K3.1 at `:411` likewise says “a question set built then.” **Cannot tell** whether B1.2 means the earlier B1.1 artifact or a current baseline set. Claude caught this ambiguity that I missed.

6. **The added baseline’s top-five procedure is unspecified — agree with the central point of claude F9.** `CACHES.md:320` makes success depend on “top 5 results,” and `:323` says, “The benchmark had no `INDEX.md` arm; this comparison adds one.” The text does not explain how the combined `INDEX.md` plus grep route produces its ordered candidates. That limits reproducibility. I reject Claude’s description of the benchmark grep ordering separately below.

7. **`INDEX.md` alone cannot answer every structured-header question — agree with part of claude F15.** At `CACHES.md:94`, “`INDEX.md` scan or a script over frontmatter” presents alternatives for questions involving `supersedes` and `depends_on`. The deciding sibling-design line, `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md:276–277`, lists only “id, kind and one-line claim.” The frontmatter route holds; the standalone index alternative is insufficient for those links. Claude caught this distinction that I missed.

8. **The index timing is unsupported by its citation — agree with claude F5, codex F13 and opencode F5.** `CACHES.md:104–105` gives “under 0.01 s and minimal tokens” against `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md`. The research run explicitly preserves the disagreement: `DECISION-RULES.md:150–151` attributes that figure to opencode, while codex reports “time GAP and tokens GAP.” It should not appear as an established measurement from the sibling design.

9. **The 226K figure describes a particular agent experiment, not opening every grep hit — agree with claude F5.** `CACHES.md:102` says “grep plus opening every hit.” The deciding source, `sqlite-fts.claude.md:55`, describes `list_dir`, grep returning up to 30 paths, `read_doc` returning limited text, and 80 model calls per question. At `:107`, S-F13 explicitly says its test does not measure token cost. The figure itself is reported in S-F4; the procedure and the extra S-F13 attribution are wrong. Claude caught this qualification that I missed.

10. **K2 does not establish that indexed statements remain true — hold codex F5.** `CACHES.md:549–550` connects SEZA’s outdated memory to “K2 (stale never answers).” K2.1 at `:395–398` compares the index with entry files. An unchanged file containing an outdated claim can pass that check. The deciding research line, `caches-numbers/nodes/codex.md:341`, says maintenance and source checking are needed. The missing projection supports a fallback concern; it does not establish that K2 would repair outdated source content.

11. **The SEZA example does not establish an early full-text win — hold codex F6.** `CACHES.md:518–520` says “full-text wins much earlier.” The SEZA researcher’s deciding line, `nodes/seza.md:337`, says “n=5, no verdict” and records full-text misses. At `:349–350`, the researcher says the paired reading path was not measured. Large grep output motivates the B1.1 comparison; it does not establish its winner.

12. **The history-loading examples support weaker conclusions than the design states — hold codex F7.** `CACHES.md:526` describes loading history “instead of retrieving,” but `caches-numbers/nodes/web.md:110` identifies the comparator as an oracle containing only the right sessions. At `:111–120`, W-19 reports RAG against vanilla long context without establishing that keyword retrieval produced the result. **Cannot tell** the actual W-19 retriever from this extract. The design’s `:535` conclusion, “it is keyword retrieval that wins,” is unsupported by these two reported comparisons.

13. **Cursor’s file-count condition is attached to the wrong result — agree with claude F10 and codex F8.** `CACHES.md:531` associates “1,000 or more files” with the 12.5% accuracy gain. `vector.claude.md:91–93` reports average question-answering accuracy separately; the 1,000-file condition qualifies code retention. The figures should remain separate.

14. **The hybrid evidence is overstated as evidence on memory-style text — agree with claude F11 and codex F9.** `CACHES.md:212` says “On memory-style text.” The cited V-F6 explicitly concerns “code (not decisions/lessons)” at `vector.claude.md:95`; V-F7 is a chunk-RAG experiment at `:102–103`. These findings can inform the proposed policy without establishing its benefit on the intended memory corpus.

15. **The loader-cap examples omit their applicability condition — hold codex F10.** `CACHES.md:484` and `:487` say “instructions plus index” under the respective caps. The deciding source, `caches-numbers/nodes/codex.md:444`, says those budgets apply **if the index is placed in that instruction chain**; a subsequent file read is governed differently. I hold the qualification issue, not a claim that the quoted loader budgets are themselves wrong.

16. **“For memory entries all six stay open” overstates the current disagreement — agree with claude F4, codex F11 and opencode F12.** At `CACHES.md:117`, historical disagreements are presented as current open questions. B1.1 at `:323` settles comparison rather than a fixed size; B1.2 at `:343–345` states the proposed full-text-before-hybrid policy. `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md:276–277` settles the index’s listed fields. D6 is settled, D5’s layout question is settled, and D1’s routing policy is answered while actual need remains unmeasured. D2–D4 retain measurement gaps.

17. **The benchmark limitations need to accompany its interpretation — agree with codex F12 and the disclosure part of opencode F4.** The opencode builder warned at `build.opencode.stdout.md:68` that the corpus moved during its run. The run recorder at `state.md:59` records a later repaired-harness run, so “no rerun” would be wrong. Neither that record nor `CACHES.md:241–242`’s determinism statement establishes a frozen snapshot. The Claude audit at `audit-0.json:93–94` also identifies timing overhead. These caveats already appear in the unresolved table, but their relation to the adjacent benchmark conclusions should be explicit. This does not invalidate the recorded maximum.

18. **The current-scope leftovers hold — agree with claude F7, codex F16, opencode F1/F7 and agy F2/F3.** `CACHES.md:54` and `:550` count three current caches/stores, against the vision’s `:27`, “two optional indexes.” The provenance note at `CACHES.md:45–47` still describes a present routing rule and an undecided vision conflict, against `:134–136` and `:60–61`. Claude also correctly identifies the prescriptive tail at `:294`, “the full suite runs before committing,” as code-work guidance. The retained benchmark observations themselves remain permitted evidence.

19. **The summary’s hash citation remains incomplete — hold codex F15.** `CACHES.md:145–147` gives a content-hash procedure with S-F9 alone. `sqlite-fts.claude.md:80` describes newest-mtime checking; `:89` supplies SHA-256. K2.1 already distinguishes these sources at `CACHES.md:397–398`. The summary should preserve that distinction.

20. **The “Not known” corpus list omits the supplied file-memory example — hold codex F17, narrowly.** `CACHES.md:561–562` lists source code, chat memory and enterprise documents. `caches-numbers/nodes/seza.md:245–248` explicitly measures a Claude memory folder and a wider collection including memory and decision records. The remaining gap is still measurement on **this design’s active-entry corpus**; SEZA does not close it. My finding concerns the incomplete evidence description.

21. **The absence row cites the wrong finding — agree with agy F4.** `CACHES.md:93` cites V-F20. `vector.claude.md:233–238` concerns linguistic negation; `:259–260`, V-F23, explicitly says top-k cannot enumerate or prove absence. Agy caught this citation error that I missed.

22. **G-F18 does not support the repo-map row — agree with opencode F13.** `CACHES.md:98` cites it for “A map of the whole repo.” At `code-graph.opencode.stdout.md:181`, F18 describes the implementation, output and determinism of the in-repo graph tool. M18 supports avoiding a whole-graph startup dump, but that does not make G-F18 evidence for the proposed map route. Opencode caught this citation mismatch that I missed.

### Points I reject

1. **My codex F1: K2.3 unconditionally requires an unapproved vector index — disagree; withdraw.** I read the joint-rebuild sentence too strongly. `CACHES.md:140` scopes the rules to “whichever cache is built”; B1.0 at `:312–313` says a passed comparison alone builds nothing, and B1.2 separately gates vector construction. K2.3 at `:402–404` addresses coordinated rebuilding of the pair. Its wording could be more explicit about installations with one cache, but it does not establish the high-severity contradiction I claimed.

2. **Claude F3, opencode F2 and agy F5: K3.2’s miss event cannot serve full-text — disagree.** K3.1 begins at `CACHES.md:410` with “A built cache.” Full-text therefore exists when its continued value is checked, and a miss involving full-text can occur in a full-text-only installation. K3.1 says to rerun the cache’s own **comparison**; it does not require the same scheduling event as initial construction. K3.2 explicitly chooses misses and EJ asking at `:413–414`. A miss does not itself demonstrate a full-text benefit or loss, but it can trigger a fresh comparison. The claimed circularity and the mandatory remedy of matching B1.1’s event are not supported.

3. **My codex F14, claude F12 and opencode F6: “the same set as `INDEX.md`” makes the generated file authoritative — disagree.** `CACHES.md:152` first says “Index only active entries.” B4.1 at `:373–374` explicitly says, “The headers are the reference, not `INDEX.md`.” Equality with a correctly generated index is compatible with that rule. The summary does not instruct the builder to use `INDEX.md` as its membership source.

4. **Claude F15’s completeness-row objection — disagree with that part.** `CACHES.md:92` requires an exhaustive scan of “`INDEX.md` **and entry headers**.” The headers supply fields absent from the index. This route does not depend on the index carrying `covers`. The separate `:94` alternative remains a supported finding above.

5. **Claude F9’s account of the benchmark grep order — disagree.** The benchmark documentation, `bench/README.md:75`, says it ranks by “distinct terms matched, then match count per file.” `bench/results.md:26–28` defines scoring and output, not ordinary filesystem print order. Claude’s conclusion that the added index arm needs a defined procedure holds; this description of the existing benchmark does not.

6. **Opencode F10: BM citations are unresolvable because the source lacks BM IDs — disagree.** `CACHES.md:233` explicitly says “ids `BM-*` below,” and `:257–276` assigns those labels to the reported categories and measurements. They are local labels with an identifiable mapping. They are not claimed to be headings already present in `results.md`.

7. **Opencode F8: Rd3.4’s G-F5 reference is substantively ambiguous — disagree.** `CACHES.md:446` includes “failure mode 5,” which resolves to `code-graph.opencode.stdout.md:218–220`, the mistyped-path finding. Adding “drift” would improve consistency with the naming note at `CACHES.md:28–29`; the present qualified reference still identifies the evidence.

8. **Opencode F9: retaining the drift citation without another graph-study label is misleading — disagree as a finding requiring repair.** `CACHES.md:27–29` identifies the code-graph source and defines `G-F5 drift`; `:142–143` describes wrong **edges**. The snapshot vision at `:45–47` explicitly labels the graph study as analogous evidence. None of these lines presents a measured memory-cache drift rate.

9. **Opencode F11: a bare contested ID necessarily misrepresents consensus — disagree.** `CACHES.md:352–353` already discloses M6’s contested status. The citations at `:97` and `:438` do not assert unanimity. `RECOMMENDATION.md:35` and `:46` preserve the disagreements. A contested recommendation can inform an owner-accepted design; repeating its voting status at every use is not necessary to make these statements true.

10. **Claude F13: Rd4.1 excludes task journals — disagree with the asserted restriction.** `CACHES.md:453` says “its `journal/` file,” while B1.2 at `:330` refers to “agents’ journals.” `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md:266–268` defines both task and project journals. **Cannot tell** the eventual comparison runner’s discovery procedure, but the text does not explicitly restrict notes to the project-root journal or establish that task misses will be omitted.

11. **Claude F14: naming Model2Vec option A imports its incremental rebuild procedure — disagree.** `CACHES.md:227` names model/runtime options from V-F18/V-F19; it does not adopt the source’s whole option-A storage and update design. B3.1 at `:366–368` expressly forbids incremental updates and reuse of previous vectors. The source’s incremental suggestion at `vector.claude.md:292–293` does not override that accepted requirement.

12. **Claude F12’s remaining vector-summary objections — disagree as requirement contradictions.** The fifth-why narrative at `CACHES.md:208–211` describes wording mismatch and cites V-F13; B1.2 at `:333–334` supplies the exact no-shared-term definition. The vector “When” row at `:224` explicitly references B1.2, and the “Who” row at `:222` says EJ decides. “Favours hybrid” could be made more precise, but the table as a whole does not replace B1.2’s test or remove EJ’s decision.

13. **Claude and agy: discovery versus exhaustive routing is already settled — disagree.** `CACHES.md:88–89` permits discovery through caches, but `:94–95` still assigns “none” to entire structured-header and “why” question classes; `:84` says, “Everything else is the files.” Those lines do not distinguish discovering a candidate from verifying its content or enumerating all matches. The explicit completeness and absence rows settle those two cases, not the disputed boundary for every other row.

14. **Opencode: vector deduplication changes the adopted top-five results — disagree with that implication.** The Claude audit’s deciding line, `audit-0.json:52`, says, “The hit@5 tables do not change.” It reports fewer than ten returned paths, not an altered measured top-five hit rate. Other low-severity findings can affect character output: `:100–101` explicitly reports that effect for hybrid blocks. Neither statement settles the whole audit.

15. **Agy: the lower-severity findings were never addressed by code changes — cannot tell; reject that categorical claim.** `state.md:56–59` records repair of the five high findings and a later run, with re-audit stopped. That does not establish the disposition of every low finding. The design can accurately say that their closure is unverified; absence of re-audit is not proof that no repair occurred.

### My own points: held or withdrawn

- **F1 — Withdrawn:** K2.3 does not establish mandatory construction of an unapproved vector index.
- **F2 — Held:** Rd4’s blanket exclusion conflicts with B1.1’s exceptional journal note.
- **F3 — Held:** the missing-model summary has the wrong fallback order.
- **F4 — Held:** treatment of historical misses against current active entries remains unspecified.
- **F5 — Held:** cache-to-file freshness does not establish source-content truth.
- **F6 — Held:** SEZA’s broad grep output does not establish a B1.1 win.
- **F7 — Held:** oracle context selection and unspecified RAG do not establish the stated keyword-retrieval conclusion.
- **F8 — Held:** Cursor’s file-count condition belongs to retention, not average accuracy.
- **F9 — Held:** the cited hybrid studies do not establish the stated memory-text result.
- **F10 — Held:** loader-cap examples need the instruction-chain condition.
- **F11 — Held:** “all six stay open” fails to distinguish settled policy from unmeasured benefits.
- **F12 — Held, narrowed:** carry the unclosed snapshot caveat with the interpretation; do not claim the rerun never happened or the recorded determinism result is false.
- **F13 — Held:** the index timing is not measured by its cited design.
- **F14 — Withdrawn:** equality with `INDEX.md` does not make it the membership authority.
- **F15 — Held:** S-F9 does not supply the summary’s hash procedure.
- **F16 — Held:** current three-cache counts and the undecided scope-conflict wording are leftovers.
- **F17 — Held, narrowed:** the corpus list omits SEZA file memory; the gap on the designed active-entry corpus remains real.
